### figure9.png

---
TITLE:              Not bold — "Sleep spindles at three resolutions." is present and appropriately concise as the opening sentence, but it is not typeset in bold (no `\textbf{}`).

PANEL DESCRIPTIONS: Complete — (a), (b–g) with explicit mapping of window length and method to each letter pair, and (h) are all described in order. Panel labels are lowercase as required.

ERROR BARS:         Not present — no error bars or distributions are plotted. The horizontal connector lines in (h) join the two estimators at a common setting and are not uncertainty intervals; the caption should say so explicitly so they are not misread as intervals.

SAMPLE SIZE:        Missing — the caption never states the number of subjects/recordings (apparently n = 1 recording, one 40-s N2 epoch), nor the number of windows averaged/plotted per spectrogram (40 s stepped by 0.1 s ⇒ ~390–400 windows depending on edge handling). IEEE TBME requires the number of realizations/windows behind any displayed spectral estimate.

STATISTICAL DETAIL: Partial — no hypothesis test is performed, which is acceptable for a methods-illustration figure, but: (i) the equivalent degrees of freedom ν are given numerically for each panel (good, and required here since noise/variance levels are being compared) yet ν is never defined as "equivalent degrees of freedom of the estimator" until panel (h); (ii) the smoothing-parabola half-bandwidth / span is not specified; (iii) the cosine taper parameter (e.g., proportion tapered) is not given; (iv) the sampling rate, FFT length, and detrending/zero-padding are absent, so ν and the half-power widths cannot be reproduced.

ABBREVIATIONS:      Undefined: EEG (used in the (a) axis label and annotation); PSD (colour-bar label); N2 (sleep stage — expand to "NREM stage 2 (N2)"); C4-M1 (state that this is the 10–20 right central derivation referenced to the left mastoid); dB re 1 µV²/Hz (state reference explicitly in caption); R (appears in the (h) row labels "1 s, R = 2 Hz" but is never defined); α (used in "α = 1", "L = 2α − 1", "at α = 2" but never defined as the time–half-bandwidth product NW); L (implied as taper count, should be stated as "number of Slepian/DPSS tapers"); ν (defined only obliquely).

COLOR/SYMBOL KEY:   Present — black vs. grey traces, grey shading for spindles, the white resolution box, the shared colour bar, and blue circles vs. orange squares in (h) are all explained. Missing only: whether the colour scale is clipped/saturated (large dark-red regions below ~3 Hz suggest values exceed the 13 dB ceiling).

SELF-CONTAINED:     No — a reader cannot reproduce or fully interpret the panels without the main text. Missing: definition of α, R, L, ν; sampling rate and FFT/padding details; smoothing-parabola span; cosine-taper fraction; number of windows; subject/dataset provenance and ethics/consent statement; whether the colour scale is clipped.

ISSUES:
  - [BLOCKING] Number of windows (realizations) behind each spectrogram estimate is not stated, as required when noise levels/variance of estimators are compared.
  - [BLOCKING] Symbols appearing in the figure but undefined in the caption: R, α, L, ν (as equivalent degrees of freedom), PSD, EEG, N2. R is especially problematic because it labels the y-axis categories of panel (h).
  - [BLOCKING] Data provenance and sample size absent: one subject? one epoch? which dataset/recording? No statement that this is a single illustrative epoch.
  - [BLOCKING] Estimator parameters needed to reproduce the stated half-power widths and ν are missing: sampling rate, FFT length/zero-padding, cosine-taper fraction, parabolic-smoother half-bandwidth.
  - [ADVISORY] The horizontal connectors in (h) could be mistaken for error bars/intervals; state explicitly that they merely link the two estimators at a common window/resolution setting and that no uncertainty is plotted.
  - [ADVISORY] Colour scale appears saturated at the upper end (extensive uniform dark red below ~3 Hz). If values are clipped, say so; otherwise readers will misjudge low-frequency power.
  - [ADVISORY] In-panel header strings ("Multitaper, L = 3: 2 s by 1.7 Hz, ν = 6.0", etc.) function as panel titles, which IEEE TBME discourages inside the figure. They are informative and compactly placed, so retaining them is defensible, but consider relabelling as annotations (e.g., "MT, L = 3") and moving full specifications to the caption.
  - [ADVISORY] The last two sentences ("At 1 s, α = 1 … long windows do the opposite.") are interpretation/discussion rather than description; trim to one short takeaway sentence and move the rest to the main text.
  - [ADVISORY] Resolution check: 2002 px across a 181 mm double-column width ≈ 281 DPI, marginally below the 300 DPI minimum for colour line art/raster composites. Re-export at ≥300 DPI (≥2140 px wide) as TIFF/EPS/PDF for final submission; current 1.79 MB PNG is fine for review only.
  - [ADVISORY] Verify that all in-figure text (tick labels, the "resolution of the panel" annotation, and (h) value labels) is ≥6 pt — preferably 8 pt — at 181 mm final width; the small annotations in (b) and the value labels in (h) are the ones at risk.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (a) has no y-axis note that the 11–16 Hz trace is offset by a stated amount and is not to the same amplitude scale; the caption says "offset" but not by how much or whether the gain is identical.

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - Bold the opening title: `\textbf{Sleep spindles at three time–frequency resolutions.}`
  - Add a definitions clause early: "EEG, electroencephalogram; PSD, power spectral density; N2, NREM stage 2; C4-M1, right central derivation referenced to the left mastoid (10–20 system). α denotes the time–half-bandwidth product NW, L the number of Slepian (DPSS) tapers, R the target half-power resolution, and ν the equivalent degrees of freedom of the estimator."
  - Add provenance and sample size: "Single illustrative 40-s epoch from one participant (n = 1 recording; dataset/cohort name, ethics approval no.). Sampled at XXX Hz."
  - Add window count and estimator settings: "Each spectrogram comprises N = ___ windows (40 s stepped by 0.1 s); FFT length ___ with ___ zero-padding; cosine taper with ___ % of the record tapered at each end; parabolic smoother of half-bandwidth ___ Hz. ν values quoted per panel are the equivalent degrees of freedom of the corresponding single-window estimate."
  - Clarify (h): "Markers give the achieved half-power width and ν for the multitaper estimate (blue circles) and the smoothed cosine-tapered periodogram (orange squares); grey lines connect the two estimators at the same window length and target resolution R and do not represent uncertainty. No error bars are shown."
  - State colour-scale handling: "All panels share the colour bar; values above 13.5 dB re 1 µV²/Hz are clipped to the maximum colour." (Or extend the scale.)
  - Add explicit amplitude information for (a): "the 11–16 Hz trace is plotted at the same gain, offset by −100 µV; grey vertical bands mark five visually scored spindles."
  - Trim the interpretive closing to one sentence, e.g., "Short windows preserve spindle timing but blur them in frequency; long windows do the reverse," and move the α = 1 versus α = 2 discussion to the main text.
  - Re-export at ≥300 DPI (600 DPI preferred) in RGB as TIFF/EPS/PDF, keeping the file under 10 MB, and confirm minimum 6 pt (target 8 pt) font at 181 mm width.

## Statistical Integrity Checker

### efigure1.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — This is a methods/theory figure displaying deterministic mathematical objects (taper sequences, eigenvectors of $Q$, eigenvalue-derived weights, and spectral concentration kernels), not sampled empirical data. Line plots for (a–h) and (m–p) are the correct choice. The bar charts in (i–l) are also acceptable here: each bar is a single exact, non-stochastic quantity ($c_i/\sum c_i$), so there is no underlying distribution being concealed and no expectation of overlaid individual points. A stem/lollipop plot would arguably be a slightly better idiom for an indexed discrete spectrum (it avoids implying the bar *area* carries meaning), but this is stylistic, not an integrity issue.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Visible (by analogy) — No statistical $n$ exists. The relevant analogues are fully recoverable from the figure: $N=256$ (from the $k$ axes), the number of segments/tapers is directly countable as bars in (i–l) (7, 12, 10, 7), $L=7$ is titled in (d), the shift set $n=0,32,\dots,192$ is annotated in (a), and the equivalent degrees of freedom $\nu$ is printed in each of (i–l). This is exemplary disclosure of the parameters that determine the displayed quantities.

**COMPARISON:** Valid — The four columns are matched one-to-one across rows, and the shared y-limits within rows (e–h) and (m–p) make the cross-method comparison honest. Panel (c) is the one exception: it is normalised by $\max h$ and spans 0–1, whereas (a), (b), (d) are in absolute taper units on different scales. This is defensible (the sliding sinc has a very different amplitude scale), but it means row 1 is not visually commensurable and a reader skimming may wrongly infer that the sinc window has far larger amplitude. Worth an explicit note.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** None — Bar panels (i–l) all begin at exactly 0.00 and share identical 0–0.20 limits, so relative weight magnitudes across methods are read correctly. The dB panels are ratio scales anchored at 0 dB (peak-relative) with a common −80 dB floor; the −80 dB cut does clip the deep nulls in (m), but since a logarithmic magnitude scale has no true floor, some clip is unavoidable and the choice does not exaggerate any difference of interest.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present (mild, cosmetic) — In (e) the four eigen-tapers cross repeatedly and the mid-tone pinks ($i=2$ vs $i=4$) are hard to separate where they intersect near $k\approx60$ and $k\approx200$; the same applies to the two mid oranges in (f). In (o) and (p) a dotted and a solid curve appear to lie almost exactly on top of one another over the passband, and it is not stated in the caption what the second (dotted/overplotted) trace is. No density information is lost — these are smooth deterministic curves — so this is legibility rather than data concealment.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No hypothesis tests, p-values, or significance annotations appear anywhere in the figure.

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable — The $k$ axis indexes sample position within a fixed-length window, not longitudinal observation times; all sequences are fully sampled on $0\le k\le 256$ with no gaps, and no interpolation across missing points occurs.

**ISSUES:**
- [ADVISORY] Row 1 mixes scales: (c) is normalised to $\max h$ while (a), (b), (d) are unnormalised, and the caption does not warn the reader. Invites a false amplitude comparison across the row.
- [ADVISORY] Legends are incomplete/inconsistent. The $i=1\ldots4$ colour key appears only in (d) and (e); (f), (g), (h) rely on the reader carrying it across panels. Similarly (b) has its own $f'N\Delta$ key that uses the same orange ramp as (f)/(j) for a *different* variable, which risks conflation.
- [ADVISORY] In (m–p) an apparent second (dotted) curve nearly coincides with the solid kernel but is never identified in the caption; the only dotted elements the caption defines are the vertical lines at $|f|=R/2$.
- [ADVISORY] Caption reports four half-power widths as "5.84, 8.03, 8.03 and $7.41/(N\Delta)$" with the unit attached only to the last value, and the out-of-band power figures (0.05%, 0.13%, 1.39%, 0.22%) are not marked on the plots, so they cannot be checked against (m–p) by eye.
- [ADVISORY] (i–l) have unequal numbers of bars (7, 12, 10, 7) with no annotation of why the taper count differs by method; the reader must infer it from the construction.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Add to the caption an explicit statement that (c) is normalised by $\max h$ whereas (a), (b) and (d) are not, or add a scale annotation inside (c).
- Identify the dotted curve in (m–p) in the caption, or if it is a duplicate/overlay of the same kernel, remove it; if it is a theoretical target, label it in a small legend in (m).
- Replicate the four-colour $i$ legend in at least one panel per row, or state once in the caption that the shade ordering (dark → light = $i=1\to4$) holds throughout, and use a visibly distinct linestyle for $i=2$ vs $i=4$ in (e) and (f) to resolve the crossing ambiguity.
- Attach the unit $1/(N\Delta)$ to all four half-power widths in the caption, and consider printing each panel's half-power width and out-of-band fraction inside (m–p) so the quoted numbers are verifiable against the plotted kernels.
- Annotate the number of segments/tapers ($L$ or equivalent) inside each of (i–l) alongside the existing $\nu$ value, so the differing bar counts are self-explanatory.
- Optional: render (i–l) as stem/lollipop markers rather than filled bars, since the abscissa is a discrete index and bar area carries no meaning.
- Consider an inset or broken axis in (m) if the depth of the nulls below −80 dB is scientifically relevant, since they are currently clipped at the axis floor.

### efigure2.png

---

**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — Overlaid line plots of PSD (dB) and of estimate-minus-truth versus normalized frequency are the conventional and correct display for spectral estimator comparisons. No bar charts, pie charts, or error-bar-on-mean constructions are present, so the distribution-hiding concerns of the checklist do not apply. Panel (b) is a well-chosen difference (residual) panel rather than a second overlay, which is good practice.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible (in figure) — The caption gives $N=1024$ samples and states this is *one realization* of the AR(4) process, but neither $N$ nor the number of realizations is annotated in the figure itself. Effective degrees of freedom ($\nu$) per estimator — arguably the relevant "n" for a spectral estimate — appear only in the caption. For a single realization, the "individual data points" are in effect fully shown (every frequency bin is plotted), so there is no concealment of raw data; the concern is instead that $n_{\text{realizations}}=1$ cannot support distributional claims.

**COMPARISON:** Valid, with one concern — All estimators are computed from the *same* realization, i.e. the comparison is intrinsically paired, and panel (b) correctly exploits this by plotting per-frequency differences against a common truth. This is the right analogue of a paired-difference plot. The concern is inferential, not graphical: the caption's quantitative claims ("18 dB above the true PSD in the median", "lowers that to 4 dB") are median-over-frequency statistics from a single draw, and frequency bins are not independent (smoothing over bandwidth $R$ induces correlation). These numbers therefore carry sampling variability that the figure does not display.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** Present (non-misleading in (a); wasteful in (b)) — In panel (a) the deepest periodogram and adaptive-multitaper excursions run below the lowest labelled tick ($-20$ dB) and appear to be clipped at the axis boundary near $-30$ to $-32$ dB; the reader cannot tell how deep the nulls actually go. In panel (b) the axis spans $-10$ to $+30$ dB while the data occupy roughly $-8$ to $+19$ dB, leaving ~11 dB of empty headroom that compresses the curves. Neither truncation exaggerates a difference (the direction of the distortion in (b) is to *understate* separation), so this is not the misleading bar-chart pattern, but both should be tightened or acknowledged.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present — This is the figure's principal weakness. In panel (b) the rapidly oscillating dark-blue ($L=7$, adaptive) and blue ($L=7$) traces sweep through 15–20 dB within a few bins, producing solid ink blocks in which the light-blue ($L=6$) and orange (cosine/parabola) curves are largely buried above $f\Delta \approx 0.28$. Which curve is visible is determined by draw order rather than by the data. The same applies to the grey periodogram behind the estimators in panel (a) between $f\Delta \approx 0.2$ and $0.5$. The reader cannot verify the caption's claim that the $L=6$ estimate sits near $+4$ dB, because its trace is occluded over exactly the frequency range in question.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No pairwise significance annotations, brackets, asterisks, or p-values appear; the comparison is deterministic-versus-truth, not inferential testing. No correction applies.

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable — The abscissa is normalized frequency on a uniform grid from 0 to the Nyquist limit 0.5, with no gaps, no missing points, and no interpolation across absent data. No integrity issue.

**ISSUES:**
- [ADVISORY] Occlusion in panel (b): the high-variance $L=7$ traces obscure the $L=6$ and cosine-taper curves over $f\Delta>0.28$, the exact region the caption's quantitative claims concern. The figure cannot presently be used to check those claims.
- [ADVISORY] Single realization ($n=1$) supports median-bias claims stated to 1 dB precision. Bias differences of 18 vs 10 vs 4 dB are plausibly robust, but the figure provides no indication of realization-to-realization variability.
- [ADVISORY] Apparent clipping of deep spectral nulls at the lower axis boundary of panel (a); the true depth of the periodogram and adaptive-estimate excursions is unknown to the reader.
- [ADVISORY] Three blues ($L=7$, $L=6$, $L=7$ adaptive) are the key contrast of the entire figure and are the least separable pair-set for deuteranopic/protanopic readers and in grayscale reproduction.
- [ADVISORY] Inconsistent annotation style between panels: panel (a) uses a boxed legend, panel (b) uses direct end-of-line labels. Also, $N$, $R$, and each $\nu$ live only in the caption.
- [ADVISORY] The grey periodogram appears in (a) but is silently dropped from (b); the caption says "each smoothed estimate", which is technically correct but the omission deserves an explicit word since the periodogram is the reference case for the leakage argument.
- [ADVISORY] Panel (b) $y$-range extends 11 dB above the maximum plotted value, needlessly compressing the traces.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Add, in panel (b), a heavy median-bias line (or a running median over a stated bandwidth) for each estimator on top of the raw difference traces, and mark the $f\Delta\ge0.4$ band used for the quoted medians. This makes the 18 / 10 / 4 dB claims directly legible despite the oscillations.
- Reduce occlusion: plot the raw difference traces with alpha ≈ 0.35 and the median overlays opaque; alternatively split panel (b) into a small-multiple column (one row per estimator, shared axes) with a light grey reference trace of the others behind each.
- Reorder draw calls so the two lowest-variance curves (cosine/parabola, $L=6$) are drawn last, or draw the $L=7$ traces first with reduced linewidth.
- Tighten panel (b) to approximately $-10$ to $+20$ dB, and either extend panel (a)'s lower limit to contain the full excursions or state in the caption that values below the axis are clipped.
- Replace the three-blue scheme with a colorblind-safe, luminance-ordered palette and/or add linestyle differentiation (e.g. solid / dashed / dash-dot) for the three multitaper variants, so the key contrast survives grayscale printing.
- Annotate $N=1024$ and $R=8/(N\Delta)$ inside panel (a), and append the $\nu$ value to each legend/label entry (e.g. "Multitaper, $L=7$ ($\nu=14$)") so the resolution-versus-variance tradeoff is readable without the caption.
- Strengthen the evidentiary basis for the median-bias numbers: either add a supplementary panel showing the median (and 5th–95th percentile band) of the bias across $\ge 200$ independent realizations, or state in the caption that the quoted values are from this single realization and give the across-realization spread in the text. Note explicitly that neighbouring frequency bins are correlated over the smoothing bandwidth, so the effective number of independent bins in $f\Delta\ge0.4$ is far smaller than the number plotted.
- Add one clause to the caption noting that the raw periodogram is omitted from panel (b) for legibility.

### efigure3.png

---
**Context note:** This is a deterministic/analytic figure — spectral window (kernel) functions evaluated exactly from closed-form or FFT computation. There are no sampled observations, no replicates, and no inferential comparisons. Consequently most of the statistical-visualization criteria (plot type vs. distribution, sample size display, paired comparisons, multiple-comparison correction) are **not applicable**. The review below is restricted to the criteria that do bear on this figure: axis handling, curve overplotting/legibility, and verifiability of the numbers asserted in the caption.

PLOT TYPE:          Appropriate — Overlaid line plots are the correct and conventional presentation for smooth analytic transfer functions. Linear-scale main-lobe panel plus dB-scale skirt panel is the standard two-panel idiom and is well chosen: neither scale alone would show both the plateau ripple and the 50+ dB skirt range. No bar/pie/error-bar concerns arise.

SAMPLE SIZE:        Not applicable (analog present in caption only) — There is no statistical n. The closest analogues, the series length $N=256$ and the design resolution $R=8/(N\Delta)$, appear only in the caption; they are partially inferable from the figure (the ideal box spans $|fN\Delta|\le 4$, and its height $0.125=1/8$ is consistent with $R=8/(N\Delta)$ under the stated normalisation), but $N$ itself is not recoverable from the panels. Since kernel shape depends on $N$, consider annotating $N=256$ in-panel.

COMPARISON:         Valid — All five kernels are computed at the same design resolution and plotted on common axes, which is the correct matched-condition comparison. Panel (b) is normalised to each curve's own peak, so the skirt comparison is a like-for-like relative-leakage comparison; this is stated in the caption and should be kept explicit.

AXIS TRUNCATION:    Present — Not misleading, but two items need attention. (i) Panel (a) $y$-axis begins at 0, so no exaggeration. (ii) Panel (b) is floored at $-100$ dB and the Bohman (light blue, dashed) curve runs into that floor near $|fN\Delta|\approx 20$; its true sidelobe level there is below the plotted range, so the curve appears to terminate rather than continue. A reader could misread the clipped endpoints as the achieved floor. The dotted ideal box likewise drops vertically off the bottom axis, implying infinite rejection, which is correct in principle but should be flagged as an artefact of the ideal reference rather than a computed level. (iii) The two panels use different $x$-ranges ($\pm10$ vs $\pm24$); this is justified by purpose but is easy to misread as the same window.

OVERPLOTTING:       Present — Moderate, in panel (a) only. Between roughly $|fN\Delta|<3$ the untapered (green), Hann (grey) and Bohman (light-blue dashed) plateaus coincide near 0.125 and are difficult to separate; the dashed Bohman line is legible only because of its dash pattern. The blue (multitaper) and light-blue (Bohman) hues are close in value and may not be separable in greyscale printing or for readers with colour-vision deficiency. Panel (b) is well separated and benefits from the right-hand end labels.

MULTIPLE COMPARISONS: No concern — No hypothesis tests, p-values or significance annotations are present; none are appropriate here.

TIME SERIES:        Not applicable — Axes are frequency, not time. No gaps, missing timepoints or interpolation across gaps are involved. The curves are densely evaluated and there is no evidence of undersampled interpolation, although the high-frequency multitaper ripple in (b) should be checked for adequate frequency sampling (see fixes).

ISSUES:
  - [ADVISORY] Bohman curve is clipped by the $-100$ dB axis floor; the apparent endpoint is an axis artefact, not the kernel's sidelobe level.
  - [ADVISORY] Overlapping plateaus of three kernels in panel (a) near $\mathcal{K}(f)/N\Delta=0.125$ reduce discriminability; colour pair blue/light-blue is not robust to greyscale or CVD rendering.
  - [ADVISORY] $N=256$ and $R$ are not recoverable from the panels themselves; the figure is not self-contained.
  - [ADVISORY] Quantitative claims in the caption cannot be verified from the figure at the stated precision: the skirt levels at $|f|=12/(N\Delta)$ ($-24$ dB untapered, $-75$ dB Bohman, range 51 dB) and the half-power widths ($7.4$ vs $8.0/(N\Delta)$) are not marked on the plot. Authors should confirm these against the plotted data.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (a) and (b) use different abscissa ranges without visual indication of the relationship between them.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (a) carries a legend while panel (b) uses end-of-curve labels; the redundancy is helpful but the inconsistency should be intentional and noted.

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - Extend the panel (b) $y$-axis (e.g. to $-120$ dB) or add an explicit note that curves reaching the lower limit continue below it; alternatively mark the clipped region with a hatched band.
  - Add a vertical guide line at $|fN\Delta|=12$ in panel (b) and, if space permits, tick markers or a small inset table giving the quoted skirt levels, so the caption's numbers are checkable from the figure.
  - Annotate $N=256$ and $R=8/(N\Delta)$ directly in panel (a), and mark the half-power level (e.g. a faint horizontal line at half the plateau value) so the quoted half-power widths are visually verifiable.
  - Add an inset to panel (a) magnifying the plateau region ($|fN\Delta|<3$, $\mathcal{K}/N\Delta\in[0.12,0.14]$) to resolve the coincident untapered/Hann/Bohman curves and the multitaper ripple amplitude.
  - Replace the blue/light-blue pair with a CVD-safe, greyscale-separable palette, and differentiate all five curves by line style as well as colour.
  - State the frequency sampling used for evaluation in the caption, and confirm that the multitaper ripple in (b) is adequately sampled rather than aliased by the plotting grid.
  - Consider shading or bracketing the common $\pm10$ region on panel (b) to make the relationship between the two panels' abscissa ranges explicit.
---

### efigure4.png

---

**Context note:** This figure presents *deterministic* quantities (exact kernel integrals and equivalent degrees of freedom for $N=256$), not measurements with sampling variability. Accordingly, the distribution-display, sample-size, and multiple-comparison criteria are largely inapplicable; the relevant integrity issues here are interpolation across unsampled parameter values, axis range, and curve discriminability. These are reviewed below in the standard format.

---
PLOT TYPE:          Appropriate — Connected marker-line plots of a swept control parameter against an achieved resolution are the right choice for exact, noiseless curves; markers make the actually evaluated parameter values visible, which is good practice. No bar/pie usage. Panel (b)'s log ordinate is appropriate for a quantity spanning ~6 decades.

SAMPLE SIZE:        Visible (in the applicable sense) — $N=256$ is stated in the caption but does **not** appear anywhere in the figure or axis labels; since both axes are normalised by $N\Delta$, a reader viewing the panels alone cannot recover $N$. There is no replicate $n$ to report, and the caption's explicit statement that there is "no sampling variability" correctly pre-empts any expectation of error bars — this should be retained. Individual computed points are shown as markers throughout, satisfying the spirit of the "show the data" requirement.

COMPARISON:         Valid — The core comparison design is sound and unusually well controlled: every method is plotted against its *measured* half-power width, so methods are compared at equal realised resolution rather than at equal nominal parameter, and the dotted reference at $8/(N\Delta)$ gives a common vertical slice for reading off all nine methods. One caveat: the comparison is only strictly fair along the $x$-direction if all three panels use the *same* half-power-width definition and the same swept parameter grid per method; this is implied but not stated per panel.

AXIS TRUNCATION:    None (no misleading truncation) — Panel (a) includes zero on the ordinate. Panel (c) is in dB relative to the kernel maximum, where 0 dB is the natural reference and the displayed range (−55 to ~−6 dB) is dictated by the data, not chosen to exaggerate. Panel (b)'s log axis has no floor artefact. Separate issue (not truncation but framing): all three abscissae extend to ~25 solely to accommodate two isolated Welch points, so ~35% of the plot width is empty and the densely informative region ($1.5$–$16$) is compressed. This flattens apparent differences among the eight non-Welch methods.

OVERPLOTTING:       Present (mild, localised) — Three regions are congested: (i) panel (a) for widths $\lesssim 7$, where all nine curves converge into a near-indistinguishable bundle at $\nu \lesssim 10$; (ii) panel (b) top-left, where "Untapered, then box", "Hann, then Gaussian", "Cosine taper, then parabola" and "Welch" overlap within a single decade near $10^{-2}$; (iii) panel (c) top edge, where the Hann-then-Gaussian and Welch curves are nearly coincident at ~−7 dB across the full width. Compounding this, the two blues ("Multitaper, $L=2\alpha-1$" solid, and "Bohman, then box") are close in hue and are separated mainly by line style and open-square vs open-circle markers, which will not survive greyscale printing or common colour-vision deficiencies.

MULTIPLE COMPARISONS: No concern — No inferential annotations, $p$-values, or significance brackets appear, and none would be meaningful for exact computations. Nothing to correct or verify.

TIME SERIES:        Not applicable (but the analogous interpolation concern applies) — The abscissa is an achieved resolution driven by a swept parameter, not time; however, the same "do not interpolate across gaps" standard applies, and it is violated in places (see ISSUES).

ISSUES:
  - [BLOCKING] Long straight-line interpolation across unsampled parameter gaps for the Welch curve. In panel (a) the pink line runs from $(\approx 16, \nu \approx 38.5)$ directly to $(\approx 24.5, \nu \approx 60)$, and in panels (b) and (c) it runs flat across the same ~8.5-unit gap with no intervening markers. Because Welch's segment count is integer-valued, intermediate half-power widths in that interval are largely unattainable, so the connecting segment depicts states that do not exist. The same objection applies, more weakly, to the sparse right-hand ends of "Hann, then Gaussian" (panel a) and "Cosine taper, then parabola" (panel b).
  - [ADVISORY] Integer-valued sweep parameters (number of Slepian/sine tapers $L$, number of Welch segments, box/parabola smoother half-width in bins) are rendered as continuous curves throughout. Readers may interpolate to a resolution that no setting achieves, or infer a smooth trade-off where the attainable set is a discrete lattice.
  - [ADVISORY] $N=256$ appears only in the caption, not in the figure; with both axes normalised the panels are not self-describing.
  - [ADVISORY] Abscissa range inflated to ~25 by two isolated Welch points, compressing the region where eight of nine methods live and visually understating their separation.
  - [ADVISORY] Curve discriminability: two similar blues distinguished chiefly by dash pattern and open-marker shape; three near-coincident curves at the top of panels (b) and (c); dense convergent bundle at low widths in panel (a). Not robust to greyscale reproduction or deuteranopia.
  - [ADVISORY] The filled-marker convention in panel (c) (no flat top ⇒ main-lobe skirt, not a side lobe) is explained in an in-panel note and the caption, but it silently changes the *meaning* of the plotted quantity between curves within one panel. This is an interpretive hazard that a legend entry, not a footnote, should carry.
  - [ADVISORY] The legend occupies the vacant fourth quadrant, which is efficient, but it is unlabelled and spatially detached from all three panels; nothing indicates it governs all of them.
  - [ADVISORY] Panels (a)–(c) share an abscissa but each carries its own tick labels and axis title, and the panels are not vertically aligned as a column, so the common $x$ mapping and the alignment of the $8/(N\Delta)$ reference line across panels must be verified by eye.

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - For every curve, plot markers only at computed parameter values and either (i) drop the connecting segment across the Welch gap, (ii) render gap-spanning segments in a lighter tint or dotted style reserved for "no intermediate settings exist", or (iii) add the intervening Welch points if they are computable. State explicitly in the caption that lines are visual guides between attainable settings.
  - Add a short caption or in-panel sentence noting which parameters are integer-valued, e.g. "$L$, the Welch segment count, and smoother half-widths are integers, so only the marked points are attainable."
  - Put $N=256$ into the figure itself — in a panel (a) corner annotation or by writing the axis title as "Half-power width $\times N\Delta$ ($N=256$)".
  - Either clip all three abscissae to ~17 and mark the two off-scale Welch points with an arrow and value annotation, or use a broken axis, or add a magnified inset of the $1.5$–$8$ region in panel (a) where the nine curves converge.
  - Re-map colours to a palette that is distinguishable in greyscale and under deuteranopia — in particular, move "Bohman, then box" off the blue family (e.g. purple or brown) so the three multitaper variants can own blue as a family, which also reinforces the grouping.
  - Where curves nearly coincide (panel b top-left; panel c top edge), add direct end-of-line labels so identity does not depend on resolving four legend keys.
  - Encode the flat-top distinction in the legend itself: append "(skirt, not side lobe)" to the Gaussian, parabola and Welch entries, and keep the filled/open marker convention as a redundant cue.
  - Align (a), (b), (c) on a shared abscissa with a single bottom axis title, or at minimum confirm that the dotted $8/(N\Delta)$ line sits at an identical data coordinate in all three panels; label that line once rather than relying on the panel (a) annotation alone.
  - Consider stating in the caption that the half-power width is measured identically in all three panels and from the same kernel, so the equal-resolution comparison is exact rather than approximate.
---

### figure1.png

---

**Context note:** This is a numerical/methodological verification figure (spectral estimator equivalence), not a group-comparison figure. Several checklist items (bar-graph distribution hiding, paired-data visualization, multiplicity annotation) are structurally inapplicable. The items that *do* bind here are sample-size transparency, overplotting, guide-line/axis honesty, and interpolation across sparsely sampled conditions.

---
PLOT TYPE:          Appropriate — Line traces for PSD vs. frequency (a) and for |difference| vs. frequency (b) are the correct encoding for a densely sampled deterministic function; no bar charts, pie charts, or error-bar-hidden distributions are used. Panel (c) is a small-n dot plot with markers *visible* at every sampled condition, which is exactly the recommended practice. One residual risk: in (a) the three curves are deliberately superimposed, so the figure alone cannot demonstrate that three curves are actually plotted (the reader sees essentially one trace). This is mitigated by panel (b) but should be acknowledged rather than left to the reader.

SAMPLE SIZE:        Not visible — The figure carries no n annotation. From the caption, n = 1 channel × 1 epoch × 1 subject (N = 1024 samples). Because the claim is an algebraic/numerical identity rather than a population inference, n = 1 record is defensible; however, *nothing in the figure itself* tells the reader this, and a reader skimming panel (b) could mistake the near-machine-precision agreement for a result established across many records. Panel (c) individual points (5 conditions, N_s/N = 1, 2, 4, 8, 16) are shown — good.

COMPARISON:         Valid — All three estimates in (a) and all differences in (b) are computed from the *same* record, i.e. the comparison is intrinsically paired, and the paired structure is correctly preserved by plotting differences (panel b) rather than two separate summary values. This is the right treatment of paired data. Panel (c) is likewise a within-record sweep over N_s, and the connecting line correctly signals that the points are paired across conditions rather than independent groups.

AXIS TRUNCATION:    None — (a) dB ordinate spans roughly −5 to +25 dB; dB is already a logarithmic ratio scale with no meaningful zero, so a non-zero baseline is not a truncation artifact. (b) and (c) use log ordinates, which is mandatory given the ~10¹⁵ dynamic range in (b); log axes are not "truncated" in the misleading-bar-chart sense. No exaggeration of differences detected. Minor: (b) spans 10⁻¹⁵ to ~10⁰ with gridlines only at 10⁰, 10⁻⁵, 10⁻¹⁰, 10⁻¹⁵ — decade minor ticks would help the reader read off the quoted 3×10⁻¹³ dB.

OVERPLOTTING:       Present — Two distinct instances. (i) In (a), three curves are drawn on top of one another by design; the thick blue stroke with dashed and dotted greens over it is the standard remedy and is applied competently, but at this reproduction size the dashed and dotted greens are hard to distinguish from each other, so the "two greens mark one estimator by two routes" point is not visually recoverable. (ii) In (b), the dark-green upper trace is a high-frequency oscillation sampled at ~1000+ frequency bins compressed into ~900 px, producing a near-solid ink band in which the density of excursions and the true envelope are unreadable; the deep downward spikes are individually resolved but the band interior is saturated. The lower light-green rounding-error trace is similarly saturated.

MULTIPLE COMPARISONS: No concern — No significance annotations, p-values, or inferential brackets appear anywhere in the figure; nothing to correct. No action required.

TIME SERIES:        Not applicable — Panels (a) and (b) are frequency-domain, not longitudinal. Panel (c) is a parameter sweep, not a time series; its abscissa is log-spaced in powers of two, so the geometric spacing is regular on the plotted scale and no gap is hidden. However, see the interpolation advisory below: the solid line joining five sampled N_s values asserts behaviour at unsampled intermediate window lengths.

ISSUES:
  - [ADVISORY] Sample size is not inferable from the figure. That the entire figure rests on a single channel, single 8 s epoch, single subject is discoverable only in the caption. Readers should be able to see the evidential base without leaving the axes.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (a) cannot, on its own, substantiate its own legend: three entries are claimed but one trace is perceptible. The dashed vs. dotted distinction between the two green estimators is below reliable discrimination at print size, which specifically undercuts the caption's claim that "the two greens mark one estimator reached by two routes."
  - [ADVISORY] Overplotting saturation in panel (b). The upper trace's interior is solid ink; a reader cannot judge whether the sliding-window error is uniformly distributed about ~10⁻² dB or is structured in frequency. The quoted summary statistic (0.06 dB max) is therefore not verifiable against the plot, and the reader must take the caption's word for the maximum.
  - [ADVISORY] The 1/N_s guide line in (c) has an arbitrary and unstated vertical normalisation. As drawn, it is anchored near the N_s/N = 1 datum, and the data then fall *below* it, ending roughly 40% low at N_s/N = 16. A reader cannot tell whether this indicates genuinely faster-than-1/N_s convergence, a normalisation choice, or the last point approaching a numerical floor. A dotted reference line whose offset is chosen by the author can make convergence look arbitrarily good or bad.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (c) interpolates linearly (in log–log) between only five sampled N_s values. The solid connecting line implies monotone power-law behaviour at intermediate window lengths that were never computed. With five points, the markers alone carry the evidence.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (b) presents |difference| on a log axis, which discards the sign of the discrepancy. If the sliding-window error is systematically biased (e.g. consistently low across the band) rather than zero-mean oscillatory, that structure is invisible here, yet it is scientifically the more interesting possibility.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (b)'s lower trace is labelled "rounding error" — an interpretation, not an observation. The figure asserts the identification without showing the supporting evidence (e.g. that the floor scales with machine epsilon or shifts with precision).

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - Add "single channel, single 8 s epoch; N = 1024" (and the subject count) as a small in-panel annotation in (a), so the evidential base is visible without the caption.
  - In (a), inset a magnified window over a narrow frequency span (e.g. 7–9 Hz, a few bins wide) where the three traces are separated enough to be individually counted; alternatively increase the dotted trace's dash period and reduce the blue linewidth so all three strokes are simultaneously resolvable at print size.
  - For (b), either decimate/thin the plotted trace, reduce stroke width to hairline with partial transparency, or overlay a running-maximum and running-median envelope on the raw trace so the band's internal density and the quoted 0.06 dB maximum are both readable. Mark the location of the reported maximum with a single marker.
  - Add minor decade ticks to the (b) ordinate and a horizontal reference line at the quoted 3×10⁻¹³ dB and 0.06 dB levels, so the caption's numbers are checkable against the plot.
  - State the 1/N_s guide line's normalisation explicitly in the caption (which point it is anchored to, or the fitted prefactor), and consider reporting the fitted log–log slope with its uncertainty instead of, or alongside, an eyeballed guide line. If the final point is limited by a numerical floor, annotate that floor.
  - In (c), drop the solid connecting line or render it as a faint guide, so the five computed conditions are not read as a continuum; alternatively add the intermediate N_s values if they are cheap to compute.
  - Consider a supplementary signed-difference panel (linear or symlog ordinate) for the sliding-window error, to establish whether the residual is zero-mean or biased.
  - Support the "rounding error" label with a brief statement of the evidence (e.g. that the floor tracks machine epsilon, or that it moves under a precision change), or soften the label to "at the arithmetic floor."
  - Recommended but not blocking: repeat the panel (b)/(c) verification on a handful of additional channels or epochs and report the range of maximum differences, so the agreement is shown to be a property of the estimators rather than of one record.
---

### figure2.png

---

**Context note:** This is a methods/simulation figure (spectral estimates and their expected-value kernels from a single synthetic realization), not a group-comparison figure. Several checklist items (pie/bar risks, paired-group plots, multiple-comparison annotation) are structurally inapplicable. The items that *do* transfer — realization count visibility, axis clipping/truncation, ink-saturation overplotting, and inset-scale comparability — are assessed below.

---
PLOT TYPE:          Appropriate — Line plots over a continuous frequency axis are the correct encoding for PSD estimates and kernel responses. The 4×2 small-multiple layout with shared axes supports the intended estimator-to-kernel pairing. No categorical summary plot (bar/pie) is used, so distribution-hiding risks do not apply. Minor: in (a)–(d) the "true PSD" black curve and the estimate are distinguished by colour only (grey/orange vs. black); consider also a dash pattern for the truth so the comparison survives greyscale printing and colour-vision deficiency.

SAMPLE SIZE:        Partially visible — $N=1024$ is stated in the caption but appears nowhere in the figure; the number of *realizations* (one) is the more consequential quantity and is likewise caption-only. Because a single realization is plotted, panels (a)–(d) show one draw from a sampling distribution whose variance is exactly what the $\nu$ annotations quantify. The $\nu$ values are shown in-panel, which is good practice and partially compensates. Recommend adding "$N=1024$, 1 realization" to panel (a) so the figure is self-contained when reproduced in a talk or review summary.

COMPARISON:         Valid, with one disclosure gap — The four estimators are (as the caption implies) applied to the *same* realization, which is the paired design appropriate here and avoids confounding estimator differences with realization noise. This pairing should be stated explicitly ("the same realization in (a)–(d)") rather than left to inference from "One realization." Also note that the apparent superiority of (c) is demonstrated on one draw; a reader cannot distinguish systematic bias/variance behaviour from a favourable draw. If not shown elsewhere in the manuscript, consider a supplementary panel with a modest ensemble (e.g. 100 realizations as faint traces or a pointwise 5–95% band) to establish that (c) is typically rather than incidentally well-behaved.

AXIS TRUNCATION:    Present (clipping, not exaggerating) — Panel (b) clearly has orange excursions running off the bottom of the $-40$ dB limit; deep periodogram nulls are being cut off. Panel (f) likewise appears to reach the $-150$ dB floor. This is conventional for dB spectra and does *not* exaggerate any difference — if anything it understates the periodogram's dynamic range — but it is undisclosed data clipping and should be stated. Importantly, all four PSD panels share identical limits and all four kernel panels share identical limits, so no cross-panel visual inflation occurs. No baseline suppression concern, since dB scales have no meaningful zero.

OVERPLOTTING:       Present — In (a) and (b) the periodogram oscillates faster than the pixel pitch across most of the band, so the trace collapses into a solid ink band whose upper and lower edges are readable but whose interior density is not. The visual weight of this band also partly occludes the black true-PSD curve between $f\Delta\approx0.1$ and $0.2$ in (a). This is inherent to plotting a $\nu=2$ estimate at this width and is arguably part of the figure's message, but density is being lost.

MULTIPLE COMPARISONS: No concern — No pairwise tests, brackets, asterisks, or p-values are annotated. Nothing to correct.

TIME SERIES:        Not applicable — The abscissa is normalized frequency, sampled uniformly on the Fourier grid; there are no gaps, uneven intervals, or interpolation across missing epochs.

ISSUES:
  - [ADVISORY] Single realization (n = 1 draw) underpins panels (a)–(d); neither the realization count nor $N$ is visible in the figure itself, so a reader encountering the figure without the caption cannot judge how much of what they see is sampling noise.
  - [ADVISORY] Undisclosed y-axis clipping in (b) (and likely (f)): curve excursions extend below the plotted limit, so the true depth of periodogram nulls is hidden.
  - [ADVISORY] Inset x-spans differ between (e)–(g) ($0.02/\Delta$) and (h) ($0.08/\Delta$) by a factor of four. The inset tick labels do disclose this and the caption states it, but the visual impression is that (h)'s main lobe is comparable in width to (g)'s when it is ~6× wider. This is the most likely point of reader misreading in the figure.
  - [ADVISORY] Overplotting in (a)/(b) renders the rapidly oscillating periodogram as a saturated band; interior density is unreadable and the true-PSD curve is partly occluded in (a).
  - [ADVISORY] Estimate-vs-truth distinction relies on colour alone (grey/orange vs. black).
  - [ADVISORY] Pairing of panels (a)–(d) on a common realization is implied but not stated.

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - Add "$N=1024$, one realization" as in-panel text in (a), and state explicitly in the caption that (a)–(d) use the same realization.
  - Note axis clipping in the caption, e.g. "excursions below $-40$ dB in (b) are clipped by the axis limits," or extend the (b) limit far enough to contain the trace.
  - Make the (h) inset scale difference unmissable: draw a common-length reference scale bar (e.g. $0.02/\Delta$) inside all four insets, or add "4× wider span" as in-panel text in (h). Alternatively, plot all four insets on a normalized abscissa in units of $1/(N\Delta)$ so main-lobe widths are directly comparable by eye.
  - Reduce ink saturation in (a)/(b): thinner stroke (~0.4 pt) and/or ~50% alpha on the periodogram, and draw the black true-PSD curve last so it is never occluded.
  - Differentiate the true PSD by dash pattern in addition to colour.
  - If available, add a supplementary ensemble version (faint traces or pointwise 5–95% band over ≥100 realizations) to show that the (c) behaviour is typical rather than a favourable draw; the in-panel $\nu$ values are a good quantitative substitute but do not convey realization-to-realization appearance.
---

### figure3.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — This is a simulation/methods figure, not a group-comparison figure, so the bar-chart and pie-chart concerns do not arise. Panel (a) is a correct use of a line trace for an evenly sampled record; panel (b) correctly uses connected marker-on-line curves for a continuous functional relationship (RMS error vs. resolution R), with plotted markers indicating the actual computed R grid rather than pure interpolation. One residual risk: panel (b) shows only ensemble-mean curves, so the Monte Carlo sampling distribution of the error estimate is entirely invisible. A light ±1 SD (or 5–95%) ribbon around the two "Total" curves, or a thin spaghetti overlay of a subsample of realizations, would convert this from a point-estimate figure into a distribution-showing figure at essentially no cost in clarity.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible — n = 300 independent realizations appears only in the caption; nothing in the figure itself conveys it. No individual realizations or per-realization error values are shown. Because n is large (300) the omission of individual points is defensible, but n should still be inferable from the panel (e.g., "n = 300 realizations" in the plot area or in the legend text beside "Total, whole band"). Also unstated in-figure: the number of frequency bins entering each RMS average, and whether the peak-region RMS (0.09 ≤ fΔ ≤ 0.16) is averaged over far fewer bins than the whole-band RMS — if so, the dashed curve is intrinsically noisier than the solid one and the reader has no way to see that.

**COMPARISON:** Concern — The "Total, whole band" and "Total, peak region" curves are, by construction, computed from the *same* 300 realizations at the *same* R values; they are paired, but they are drawn as two independent unpaired curves. The scientific claim in the caption is about a *shift in the location of the minimum* between the two scoring bands, and that shift is precisely the quantity for which pairing gives the tightest estimate. Consider an inset or companion panel showing the paired per-realization difference (peak-region minus whole-band error, or the difference in argmin R) with its Monte Carlo spread, rather than leaving the reader to eyeball two separately plotted means. Similarly, the decomposition "Bias + Noise = Total" is asserted graphically; showing the summed grey curves overlaid on the solid black total (or a residual) would let the reader verify the additivity rather than take it on faith.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** None — Panel (b) y-axis originates at 0 dB and the x-axis at RNΔ = 0, so no differences are visually exaggerated; this is exactly right for an error magnitude. Panel (a) y-axis is symmetric about zero and spans the data. Two secondary axis issues, neither misleading in the truncation sense but both worth fixing: (i) the upper ν axis is labelled at 2, 17, 33, 49, 66, 98, 131, i.e. the step from 66 to 98 is double the preceding steps, which invites the reader to misread the mapping as nonlinear when it is in fact essentially linear (ν ≈ 2 + 2R). Label every step, or label evenly in ν and let the tick positions fall where they fall. (ii) The tick labels on panel (a)'s x-axis (0, 255, 511, 767, 1023) are sample indices, which is correct, but the caption's N = 1024 should be stated on the panel so the taper fraction (25%) is checkable against the visible taper shoulders.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present — Two distinct instances. First, in panel (a) the orange tapered record is drawn *over* the black raw record and, because the taper is unity across the central ~75% of the span, the black trace is almost entirely hidden except at the two ends. The reader therefore cannot see the raw record where it matters most for judging what the taper did, and the visual impression is of a single orange series with black fringes. Draw the raw record on top with partial transparency, or offset the two vertically, or restrict the comparison to a zoomed end segment where the taper actually acts. Second, both traces oscillate at a period of only a few pixels over a 990-px-wide panel, so the rendering is at or beyond the aliasing limit: the apparent envelope and peak amplitudes in panel (a) are partly a rasterisation artefact, and the dashed taper's "peak amplitude" scaling cannot be verified against the visible maxima. Add a zoomed inset of ~100 samples, or plot a decimated/enveloped version with the full trace in an inset.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No pairwise significance annotations are present, and none are appropriate here. However, note the related selective-inference risk: the three circled points ("too noisy", "just right", "too smooth") are chosen *after* inspecting the same 300-realization ensemble that defines the curves, so the location of the "just right" minimum at ν = 16.7 is itself an estimate with Monte Carlo uncertainty and is optimistically biased as a performance figure. State the Monte Carlo standard error on the argmin, or show that the minimum is broad enough (the caption says "broad minimum") that the choice is insensitive — the latter is a claim the figure could support directly with a spread ribbon.

**TIME SERIES:** OK — Panel (a) is a complete, evenly spaced record with no missing samples and no gaps, so there is no misleading interpolation. Panel (b) is not a time series; its abscissa is the resolution parameter, and the computed R values are made explicit by the markers, which is good practice. Minor note: the marker spacing in panel (b) is dense at small R and sparse at large R, so the smooth appearance of the dashed curve's plateau beyond RNΔ ≈ 40 rests on only ~4 evaluated points; either say so or add points.

**ISSUES:**
- [ADVISORY] n = 300 realizations is caption-only and not inferable from the figure; no measure of Monte Carlo dispersion is shown on any curve in panel (b), so the reader cannot judge whether the shift in the minimum between the two scoring bands, or the flatness of the broad minimum, exceeds sampling noise.
- [ADVISORY] Paired data (both Total curves from the same 300 realizations at the same R grid) are displayed as unpaired independent curves, discarding the pairing that would best support the figure's central claim about the location of the optimum.
- [ADVISORY] Overplotting in panel (a): the orange tapered record occludes the black raw record across the central 75% of the span, defeating the intended raw-vs-tapered comparison.
- [ADVISORY] Rendering-level aliasing in panel (a): oscillation period is a few pixels, so apparent amplitudes and envelope are partly artefactual and the taper's amplitude scaling is not visually verifiable.
- [ADVISORY] Upper ν axis is labelled at uneven increments (…49, 66, 98, 131), risking misreading of an essentially linear mapping as nonlinear.
- [ADVISORY] The number of frequency bins entering the whole-band versus peak-region RMS averages is not given, so the reader cannot tell that the dashed curve is likely the noisier of the two.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Add a shaded ±1 SD (or 5–95 percentile) ribbon around both Total curves in panel (b), computed across the 300 realizations, and annotate "n = 300 realizations" inside the panel.
- Add a small inset or second panel showing the paired per-realization difference between peak-region and whole-band error (or the distribution of the argmin R for each criterion), so the shift in the optimum is shown with its pairing intact.
- Overlay the sum of the Bias and Noise grey curves on the solid black Total, or plot the residual, to let the reader verify the claimed decomposition.
- In panel (a), redraw the raw record on top of the tapered record with alpha ≈ 0.5, or offset the two vertically with a shared scale bar, and add a zoomed inset of ~100 samples near one taper shoulder so the taper's action and the amplitude scaling are directly visible and free of aliasing.
- Label the upper ν axis at uniform increments and state N = 1024 and the taper fraction on panel (a).
- State in the caption or panel the number of frequency bins in each RMS average, and note the Monte Carlo standard error on the estimated optimal ν alongside the "just right, ν = 16.7" annotation.

### figure4.png

---
**Assessment context:** This is a deterministic/analytic figure (exactly computed Slepian tapers, spectral kernels, and concentration eigenvalues). There are no sampled observations, no replicates, no error bars, and no group comparisons, so most of the statistical-visualization integrity criteria are structurally inapplicable. Reviewing only what applies.

PLOT TYPE:          Appropriate — Line plots for continuous functions of $k$ and $f$ (panels a–c) and a marker-plus-line plot for a discrete indexed sequence (panel d) are the correct choices. No bar charts, pie charts, or distribution-hiding summaries are present; nothing here should be converted to a dot/box/violin plot because there is no distribution to display.

SAMPLE SIZE:        Not visible — but this is a definitional parameter, not a statistical $n$. The governing parameters ($N=256$, $R=8/(N\Delta)$, $\alpha=4$, and $L$ for panel c) exist only in the caption. Since every curve's shape depends entirely on these, the figure is not self-contained. Note that panel (a) does implicitly show $N$ via the $k$ axis ending at 256, and panel (d) implicitly shows $2\alpha=8$ via the dashed marker — good practice, worth extending.

COMPARISON:         Valid — Comparisons are between analytically related curves at matched abscissae. Panels (b) and (c) share the same $f N\Delta$ axis and the same normalization family, so the visual progression from individual kernels to their weighted sum is a legitimate paired-in-frequency comparison. The dotted $\pm R/2$ band markers in (b) and the dashed box in (c) provide the correct reference context. One concern: the light-to-dark colour semantics are inverted between panels — in (a) and (b) *dark* = lowest index ($i=1$), in (c) *dark* = largest $L$. A reader scanning across panels will misread the ordering.

AXIS TRUNCATION:    None — Panels (b) and (c) both begin at exactly 0 on the ordinate, so no exaggeration of kernel amplitudes. Panel (a) uses a symmetric range containing zero and no curve is clipped. Panel (d)'s logarithmic ordinate spanning $10^{-16}$ to $\sim10^{-1}$ is the only defensible choice for a quantity varying over fifteen decades and is not truncation; it is clearly labelled as $1-\lambda_i$. Minor: the (d) abscissa's last tick is 16 while the curve runs to the panel edge, so the final data point is visually clipped by the frame.

OVERPLOTTING:       None — Curve density is low; the three traces in (b) and six in (c) remain individually resolvable. In panel (a) the $h^{(1)}$ and $h^{(2)}$ curves cross near $k\approx100$ and again near $k\approx205$ where the mid-blue and dark-blue are similar in value, but they remain separable. Circle markers in (d) are well spaced.

MULTIPLE COMPARISONS: No concern — No hypothesis tests, p-values, or significance annotations appear anywhere in the figure. Nothing to correct.

TIME SERIES:        Not applicable — Panel (a)'s abscissa is a sample index within a single record, not repeated measurements over time; there are no gaps or uneven intervals. Panel (d) is an ordered discrete index, addressed below.

ISSUES:
  - [ADVISORY] Figure is not self-contained: $N$, $R$, $\alpha$, and the taper indices governing every curve live only in the caption. Readers encountering the figure in isolation (slides, figure-only browsing) cannot reconstruct the conditions.
  - [ADVISORY] Inverted colour semantics across panels — dark encodes *low* taper index in (a)/(b) but *high* $L$ in (c). High risk of misreading the ordering of the running sum.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (d) connects discrete integer-indexed eigenvalues with a continuous line, implying interpolation between non-existent intermediate indices. This is the discrete-sequence analogue of misleading interpolation; markers are present, which mitigates it, but the line invites reading off fractional $i$.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (d) final data point is clipped at the right frame edge, past the last tick at 16. Ambiguous whether the series terminates or continues.
  - [ADVISORY] The plotted quantity $\mathcal{K}(f)$ in panel (c) is named only on the axis and defined only obliquely in the caption as a running sum; the relationship between the axis label $\mathcal{K}(f)/N\Delta$ and the caption's $\sum_{i\le L}\lambda_i|H^{(i)}(f)|^2/2\alpha\Delta$ requires the reader to reconcile two normalizations.
  - [ADVISORY] The caption's quantitative claims — edge rounding over "about $1/(N\Delta)$" and a 3% reduction of the plateau — are not annotated on panel (c) and cannot be verified at the printed resolution, since the $\sim$0.004 gap between the black curve and the dashed box top is near the linewidth.

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - Annotate $N=256$, $\alpha=4$, $R=8/(N\Delta)$ directly in a corner of panel (a) or (c) so the figure stands alone.
  - Unify the colour ramp direction, or explicitly state the mapping in each legend (e.g. "darker = larger $L$") to break the conflict with (a)/(b).
  - In panel (d), either drop the connecting line and use markers only, or render it as a thin guide in a lighter tone so the discrete nature of $i$ dominates.
  - Extend the panel (d) abscissa limit to 18 and add a tick at 18 so the terminal point sits inside the frame.
  - Give the explicit definition of $\mathcal{K}(f)$ in the caption's first mention, matching the axis normalization exactly.
  - Consider a small inset or zoomed axis break on panel (c) showing the plateau region so the stated 3% depression and the $1/(N\Delta)$ edge width are visually checkable rather than caption-only assertions.

### figure5.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — Panels (a–d) are line plots of deterministic taper sequences; panels (e–h) are bar charts of exactly computed eigen-weights $c_i/\sum c_i$. Bar charts here are *not* the usual "bar-graph-hides-distribution" problem: each bar is a single exact number, not a sample mean, and the caption states "Every curve and weight is computed exactly." No error bars are present and none should be. Ordered discrete-index weights are a legitimate bar-chart use (a stem/lollipop plot would be an equally valid, slightly lighter-weight alternative, but there is no integrity issue). No pie charts.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Visible (partially) — The relevant "n" is the number of members in each taper bank, and it is largely inferable from the figure: (a) title states "7 Hann segments" and annotates shifts $n=0,32,\ldots,192$; (d) title states $L=7$; (e–h) the number of non-zero bars directly encodes bank size (7, ~12, ~10, 7). Two gaps: (b) shows four of an acknowledged *continuum* and (c) shows four of $N$ sliding positions, so the panels alone do not convey that only a subset is drawn — this is stated only in the caption. Since every quantity is exact, individual-point display requirements do not apply.

**COMPARISON:** Concern (minor, presentational) — The four routes are intended to be compared on equal footing, but the (a–d) y-axes are not commensurate: (a), (b), (d) plot amplitude in comparable units (0–0.2, ±0.1, ±0.2) while (c) plots $h_{k-n}/\max h$, a *normalized* quantity on a 0–1 scale. A reader scanning the row may misread panel (c)'s sinc as an order-of-magnitude larger taper. The (e–h) row is correctly harmonized (identical 0–0.20 y-range and shared x-range), which is good practice and should be preserved. The $\nu$ values are deterministic functionals, so no inferential comparison is being made and no significance annotation is needed — correct.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** None — All bar panels (e–h) begin at $y=0$ with a visible baseline, so bar lengths are proportional to the plotted quantity. The $\nu$ annotations are printed as text rather than encoded as bar height, avoiding any exaggeration. Line panels (a–d) use ranges appropriate to signed taper amplitudes; truncation conventions for bars do not apply to them.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present — Two places:
1. Panel (a): seven time-shifted Hann copies are drawn in a *single* shade with no lightening, so overlapping lobes are indistinguishable and the seventh copy's tail is only separable by eye at the baseline. The caption promises "lighter shades for later members of the bank in (b–d)" — (a) is explicitly excluded, creating a row inconsistency.
2. Panels (b) and (c): near the endpoints ($k\approx0$ and $k\approx256$) multiple curves converge and superimpose; in (c) the low-amplitude sidelobe structure of all four positions collapses into a single dark band near $y=0$, obscuring which curve is which.
3. Panels (e–g): the last few bars have heights $<0.01$ and are visually indistinguishable from zero / from absent bars.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No pairwise tests, p-values, or significance brackets are annotated anywhere in the figure. All displayed quantities are exact analytic/numerical results, so no correction question arises.

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable (with one advisory) — The abscissa $k$ is a discrete sample index, not time-ordered measurement with possible missingness. Continuous interpolation between integer $k$ is drawn, but with $N=256$ samples the polyline is a faithful rendering of the underlying sequence and there are no gaps to interpolate across. No misleading interpolation.

**ISSUES:**
- [ADVISORY] Panel (c) uses a normalized ordinate ($h_{k-n}/\max h$, range 0–1) while sibling panels (a), (b), (d) use unnormalized amplitude, undermining the intended like-for-like comparison across the top row.
- [ADVISORY] Panel (a) draws all seven bank members in one identical shade, breaking the "lighter shades for later members" convention used in (b–d) and making the seven overlapping copies individually untraceable.
- [ADVISORY] In (e–h) the x-axis is ticked only at 5 and 10, so the first bar cannot be confirmed as $i=1$ and the exact bank size must be counted bar-by-bar; the ladder length is a key quantity (it sets $\nu$) and deserves an unambiguous tick at $i=1$.
- [ADVISORY] In (e) and (h) the x-axis extends to $i\approx12$ with no bars past $i=7$; a reader cannot distinguish "weight is exactly zero" from "taper does not exist / not plotted." Same ambiguity for the sub-0.01 bars at the tails of (f) and (g).
- [ADVISORY] Bank size is not inferable from panels (b) and (c) alone — that only four of a continuum / of $N$ positions are shown appears solely in the caption text.
- [ADVISORY] $\nu$ is quoted to three significant figures (13.4, 16.7, 17.2, 14.0) without stating that it is an exact functional of the plotted $c_i$; a reader accustomed to empirical figures may mistake these for estimates with unreported uncertainty.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Plot panel (c) in the same amplitude units as (a), (b), (d), or, if normalization is essential for legibility, add an explicit in-panel note ("normalized") and consider a light grey band or annotation marking the unnormalized peak so the scale change is unmissable.
- Apply the same light-to-dark shade ramp in (a) that is used in (b–d), and add a short legend or shift annotation so individual Hann copies are traceable.
- Add an x tick and label at $i=1$ in (e–h) (e.g. ticks at 1, 5, 10) so the ladder index and bank size read directly off the axis.
- Mark exactly-zero / not-defined indices in (e–h) explicitly — e.g. open markers at zero, small "×" glyphs, or a light grey shaded region labelled "no further tapers" — so absent bars are not read as an axis artefact. Alternatively, truncate each panel's x-range to its own bank size and state the ranges in the caption, accepting the loss of a shared axis.
- For the sub-0.01 tail weights in (f) and (g), either add a thin inset with a magnified y-range or annotate the smallest weight numerically, so the tail of the ladder (which materially affects $\nu$) is legible.
- Consider a stem/lollipop rendering for (e–h); with a zero baseline retained it conveys the same discrete-index information with less ink and makes near-zero weights visible as markers on the axis.
- Add a brief caption or in-panel statement that all quantities are exact (no sampling variability, hence no error bars) — this pre-empts reviewer queries about missing uncertainty and clarifies why the $\nu$ values carry no intervals.
- State in the caption how many of the continuum/$N$ members are drawn in (b) and (c) directly adjacent to the panel labels, or annotate the panel itself (e.g. "4 of a continuum"), so the subsetting is apparent without reading the full caption.

### figure6.png

---
**Context note:** This is a deterministic/simulation figure (spectral estimates and kernels), not a group-comparison figure. Most items on the statistical-visualization checklist are formally not applicable, but two map onto real concerns here: (i) "sample size visibility / show the distribution" — a single Monte Carlo realization is shown in place of the sampling distribution, and (ii) "comparison validity" — the four estimators are only approximately matched in resolution.

PLOT TYPE:          Appropriate — Line overlays of estimate vs. true PSD (a–d) and kernel transfer functions with log-frequency-zoom insets (e–h) are the standard and correct displays for this material. No bar charts, pie charts, or error-bar-over-hidden-distribution constructions. The dB ordinate is the appropriate scale for quantities spanning ~10⁶ in power.

SAMPLE SIZE:        Not visible — $N=1024$, $R=8/(N\Delta)$, "one realization," and the 300-realization ensemble medians all live in the caption only; none is inferable from the figure. For panels (a–d) the relevant "n" is the number of realizations displayed, which is 1. The ensemble statistics that actually support the paper's claim (5.3, 2.3, 1.0, 1.0 dB over 300 realizations) are not rendered anywhere in the figure.

COMPARISON:         Concern — Two points. (1) All four panels show the *same* single realization, so the comparison is effectively paired, which is the right design, but nothing in the figure signals that the four curves come from one common record; adding a "same realization" annotation would make the pairing explicit and forestall the reading that four independent draws are shown. (2) The estimators are not exactly matched: half-power widths 8.2/7.2/8.2/7.8 $(N\Delta)^{-1}$ and $\nu = 17.2/14.0/16.7/17.6$. The multitaper panel (b) therefore has ~19% fewer equivalent degrees of freedom and ~12% narrower bandwidth than (a)/(c), which inflates its visible ripple relative to the others independently of leakage. This is disclosed numerically but not visually, and readers will attribute all of the extra jitter in (b) to the method.

AXIS TRUNCATION:    None — (a–d) share a common $[-40, 60]$ dB ordinate and a common $[0, 0.5]$ abscissa; (e–h) share $[-150, 0]$ dB and the insets share $[-50, 0]$ dB over $[0.39, 0.41]$. Shared limits across panels is exactly right and makes the 22 dB / 14 dB floors directly comparable. No exaggeration of differences. Minor: the (e–h) ordinate cuts panel (g)'s curve at $-140$ dB near $f\Delta = 0$ and it descends off the plotted range; confirm nothing is clipped without indication.

OVERPLOTTING:       Present (mild, unavoidable) — In panel (b) the multitaper estimate oscillates at the Rayleigh scale and the trace becomes a solid band above $f\Delta \approx 0.25$, obscuring both the local density of the oscillation and the black true-PSD curve beneath it. The same occurs to a lesser degree in (a) for $f\Delta > 0.2$. Suggest reduced line width, partial transparency for the colored trace, or plotting the true PSD *on top* of the estimate (or as a dashed white-cored line) so it remains legible where the estimate is dense.

MULTIPLE COMPARISONS: No concern — No significance annotations, no p-values, no pairwise tests. The four-way method comparison is descriptive.

TIME SERIES:        Not applicable — Frequency-domain functions on a complete, evenly spaced grid; no gaps and no interpolation across missing support.

ISSUES:
  - [BLOCKING] The figure displays a single realization that the caption itself identifies as atypical ("This realization has more leakage than is typical"; per-realization median absolute errors 11.6/4.6/0.9/0.8 dB vs. ensemble 5.3/2.3/1.0/1.0 dB — a factor of ~2 overstatement of the leakage penalty for (a) and (b)). A reader who does not parse the caption arithmetic will take panels (a) and (b) as representative. Showing an unrepresentative draw while the quantitative claim rests on an ensemble is the frequency-domain analogue of showing a hand-picked exemplar in place of the distribution, and it needs to be either corrected in the display or flagged inside the figure, not only in prose.
  - [ADVISORY] The 22 dB and 14 dB arrow annotations are labelled as medians for $f\Delta \ge 0.4$, but it is ambiguous from the figure whether they are medians of this realization or of the 300-realization ensemble (the caption says "the median for $f\Delta\ge0.4$", which reads as within-realization). Given the realization is atypical, these two headline numbers may themselves be inflated relative to ensemble values.
  - [ADVISORY] Annotation is asymmetric: only (a) and (b) carry a bias arrow. Panels (c) and (d) have no corresponding marker, so the visual evidence for "these two are fine" is qualitative while the evidence against (a)/(b) is quantitative.
  - [ADVISORY] $\nu$ and half-power width differ across panels, confounding variance/resolution with leakage; the differences are stated only as numbers in the caption and in-panel $\nu$ labels, with no visual cue.
  - [ADVISORY] $N=1024$, $R$, and the "one realization" status appear nowhere in the figure; the figure is not self-contained.
  - [ADVISORY] Overplotting of the dense multitaper trace hides the reference (true PSD) curve over roughly half the band in panel (b).

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - Either (i) replace the single trace in (a–d) with the ensemble median plus a shaded 10–90% (or 5–95%) envelope over the 300 realizations, retaining one thin realization line for texture, or (ii) if a single realization is kept deliberately to show the leakage mechanism, add a second row (or an inset per panel) with the ensemble envelope, and place an explicit in-panel tag such as "single realization (atypical leakage)" so the caveat travels with the graphic.
  - Add "$N=1024$, $R=8/(N\Delta)$, same realization in (a)–(d)" as a figure-level annotation or in a corner of panel (a).
  - Clarify in the caption whether 22 dB and 14 dB are within-realization or ensemble medians over $f\Delta\ge0.4$, and if the former, give the ensemble values alongside them.
  - Annotate (c) and (d) symmetrically, e.g. a matching arrow with "$<1$ dB", so all four panels carry the same quantitative marker.
  - Report $\nu$ and half-power width in a consistent in-panel form for all four (e.g. "$\nu=14.0$, $B=7.2/(N\Delta)$") so the reader can see that (b) is the least-smoothed estimate without consulting the caption.
  - Reduce the estimate line width to ~0.5 pt and/or apply alpha ≈ 0.7, and draw the black true PSD last (on top) so it remains visible through the dense regions of (a) and (b).
  - Confirm that panel (g)'s kernel is not silently clipped at the $-150$ dB floor; if it is, either extend the axis or add a break/clip indicator.
---

### figure7.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — Overlaid line plots are the correct form for continuous spectral estimates evaluated on a dense frequency grid; a difference panel (b) is the right complement to the overlay in (a). No bar/pie/error-bar-summary misuse. One suggestion: because the three curves nearly coincide over much of (a), consider a small inset zoom (e.g. 8–12 Hz peak + adjacent valley) so the reader can see *where* the multitaper's finer structure differs, rather than relying on (b) alone.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible — Two distinct "n"s are at play and neither is on the figure. (i) The number of frequency bins (1281) appears only in the caption; (ii) more importantly, this is a **single EEG epoch** (n = 1 record, one subject), which is nowhere stated in the figure. A reader skimming the panels could mistake the 1281 points for 1281 independent observations. Annotate the axes/panel with "one 12.15 s epoch; 1281 frequency bins" or similar. Individual data points are not applicable here (the underlying data are a continuous function, not replicate observations), so the small-n dot-plot rule does not bite — but the n = 1 record limits generalization and should be explicit.

**COMPARISON:** Valid — The three estimators are applied to the *same* epoch, i.e. the comparison is inherently paired, and panel (b) correctly visualizes the paired differences rather than only superimposing marginal curves. This is the right choice and should be preserved. Minor: state in the caption that (b) is a pointwise paired difference at identical frequencies (same grid for all three estimators — confirm the smoothed/Welch estimates were interpolated onto, or natively share, the multitaper grid; if any resampling was needed, say so, as interpolation before differencing can manufacture or suppress structure).

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** None (with one caveat) — Panel (a) does not include zero, but the ordinate is dB re 1 µV²/Hz, where zero is an arbitrary reference, so no baseline obligation exists. Panel (b) is symmetric about zero (±15 dB) with a dashed zero line and comfortably exceeds the data range (≈ −5 to +12.5 dB), so differences are if anything *under*-emphasized — commendable. Caveat: the abscissa stops at 40 Hz. If the Nyquist frequency is appreciably higher, the displayed range is a selection, and agreement/disagreement above 40 Hz is invisible. State the sampling rate and Nyquist frequency, and either extend the axis or justify the 40 Hz cut (e.g. anti-alias filter corner, band of interest).

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present — Two forms. (1) In (a) the curves are drawn in a fixed z-order (blue first, then orange, then pink); wherever the estimates agree, the later-drawn traces conceal the earlier one, so the reader cannot tell coincidence from occlusion. (2) In (b) the orange and pink traces cross and overlap repeatedly at high frequency.
